### Calibrating the length of a well-formed spoken command

The archived additive per-word length penalty is zero. Compare the frozen mean segment cost with (i) a fixed per-word penalty calibrated only on familiar three-/five-word source readings, (ii) a duration-only candidate selector, (iii) adding a duration mismatch cost to mean segment cost. Duration model fits a positive approximate number of voiced-feature frames per word handle, including a nonnegative common offset, from whole recordings without boundaries or synthesis rate input. Fit on 24 previously correctly understood three-/five-word phrases per voice, rerecorded at 191 and 225 wpm, using archived open hearings as pseudo-labels. Shrink word durations toward the common per-word mean with four pseudo-observations per coefficient. Score squared log observed/predicted duration, divided by source mean squared log residual (floor .05²); weight by median source correct candidate acoustic cost. Calibrate global length penalty from [-1,-.5,-.25,0,.25,.5,1] times that median cost per word, maximize source exact hearings breaking ties toward zero. Freeze a mixed-length 243-wpm evaluation with the prior 16 seven-word test strings, eight three- and sixteen five-word controls, preserving the 189-wpm demonstration. Require ≥80% complete decisive four-way sets on each voice, zero newly wrong short recordings relative to the baseline, and compare duration-only with the combined score. Use the opened 237-wpm panel for historical replication. All candidates use the same learned alternation and 1–8 count search; no target count, tempo or point supplied to the scorer. Source and target shorter strings overlap, longer test strings were excluded from calibration.

Diagnostic frozen before inspecting the fresh scores: pair the sixteen seven-word evaluation recordings with the sixteen five-word controls in generation order. Resample both acoustic feature sequences by linear temporal interpolation to their common rounded geometric-mean frame count. All other cues may change under this manipulation; use identical transformed features across policies. Keep originals for three-word controls and the demonstration. Test the frozen calibration without target refitting; report paired exact five-and-seven counts and known-length candidate availability, so elapsed-time discrimination can be distinguished from retaining the sounds on both sides. This is a feature-space intervention, not newly spoken audio.

At 237 and 243 wpm, calibrated per-word penalty selected zero on both voices. Duration-only and combined reranking recovered all 384 decisive sets per voice, versus 288 with mean acoustic cost, and preserved every shorter command. Both methods still use an acoustic candidate per legal count. Matched-time manipulation: all 16 five/seven pairs contained the true candidate at both lengths for each voice. Mean acoustic cost transcribed both sides in 14/16 pairs per voice; adding duration reduced this to 0/16 and lost all decisive semantic sets. On this panel the duration-only and combined methods misheard every transformed seven-word recording. Thus the apparent gain on uniform rate depends heavily on elapsed time.

Independent follow-up: replace frame count by total spectral travel (sum of Euclidean distances between successive existing feature frames). This observable measures acoustic change; hypothesize it transfers better under timing change. Keep the same nonnegative regression, shrinkage, variance floor, median-cost multiplier, candidates and original 48 shorter source recordings. Refit once using travel as response, then freeze. Compare travel-only reranking, mean acoustic cost plus travel, duration plus acoustic cost, and mean alone. Confirm on genuinely synthesized speech: pair the same 16 five-/seven-word strings at contrasting rates (five 177 and seven 269 wpm, then reversed); use 243 wpm for three-word controls and 189 for the teaching recording. No speed metadata to the chooser. Require ≥12/16 both-exact pairs and ≥80% complete decisive semantic sets in each rate assignment and voice. Report count-conditioned ceiling to identify within-length recognition failures and scores on the previously opened natural and time-matched fixtures separately.

Spectral-travel reranking (with or without the mean cost term) reached 16/16 both-exact five/seven pairs and 384/384 decisive four-way sets on each of the two synthetic voices under each contrasting natural-speed assignment. In the fast-long condition the seven-word recording differed from its slow five-word partner by −12 to +3 frames on Rishi and −7 to +6 on Tessa. Mean acoustic cost reached 14/16 pairs and 288/384 decisive sets per voice there; duration plus acoustic cost gave 0/16 and 0/384 per voice. In the reversed-speed condition mean acoustic cost was already perfect. All 16 true pairs were available in each candidate pool. The travel method also preserved all eight three-word controls. Source duration/travel models each used 48 recordings per voice (24 known strings at two rates), with no seven-word calibration or speed metadata used at inference. Feature frames represent the acoustic service output; the measured cue is summed frame-to-frame spectral movement, not a word counter supplied by the generator.

Under exact frame-count matching by feature interpolation the combined travel reader still fell to 11/16 and 7/16 pairs on Rishi and Tessa, respectively, compared with mean-cost 14/16 on each. Semantic complete sets on this manipulation 288/384 Rishi, 192/384 Tessa; elapsed-duration method zero both. This limits the positive result to genuinely synthesized speed variation in this vocabulary; the experiment did not measure independent human speech, unfamiliar strings beyond the previously held combinations, or robust invariance to resampling. Within each length all methods use the same acoustic candidates. Independently checked every paired semantic target relation, the number of pairs containing the correct candidate at both lengths and matched frame counts. External raw files and scripts duration_audio.py, duration_fit.py, duration_ground.py, check_duration.py, duration_matched.py, matched_fit.py, travel_new.py, travel_fit.py, travel_ground.py and verify_travel.py under dual-role-word. No default or core implementation change.

### Asking for a repeat when two hearings imply different actions

Use the existing frozen mean-cost and spectral-travel readers on the crossed-speed fast-long and frame-matched fixtures with equal weight. Offer one repeat of the same command, independently synthesized at 207 wpm, for .25 simulated reward units against one unit for pointing correctly. No test transcript, correct pointer, recording condition or true speed to the decision rule. Primary policy acts on the travel reading unless the two readings select different objects on the current board; compare always using mean, always using travel, always repeating, and repeating for every transcript disagreement. For a purchased repeat, use the frozen travel reader on the returned recording. Suppress the recording after payment or substitute the valid recording obtained by flipping the last connector (keep the original task and fee fixed). Use the learned atom and connector parameters and previously inferred grouping; six libraries × eight new boards per string (seeds 92100–92105) × 32 five/seven-word commands × two grouping conventions × two acoustic conditions per voice. Pair boards and initial sound across policies; allow any maximizing object. Require ≥.01 net/output over the strongest fixed reader and preserve accuracy while reducing calls vs acoustic-only inquiry in each voice. Report descriptive finite-fixture outcomes, oracle value among offered calls, break-even price and a same-recording/different-board example. Inquiry rule and access to two interpretations supplied. Scope is a new board/repeat replay of opened strings, with a trusted repetition service, not spontaneous dialogue.

Across 6144 paired outputs per voice, asking on object disagreement preserved the transcript-disagreement policy's correct counts (6144 Rishi, 6120 Tessa) while reducing calls from 864 each to 359/410. At .25 per call net/output was .98539/.97941 versus .98177/.98486 for the strongest fixed reader (mean), differences +.00362/−.00545, missing the prespecified +.01 in both. Repeating always earned .75. Fees strictly below 112/359=.3120 Rishi and 69/410=.1683 Tessa beat the strongest fixed free reader on this mixture; these are sensitivity calculations on a fixed policy. On the fast-long half the travel reading was already correct throughout; all 57/69 purchased repeats were redundant. On the matched half the repeat corrected 243/337 otherwise wrong travel-based actions. Withholding purchased audio kept charges fixed and reduced total correct to 5901/5783; substituting the other last connector yielded 5931/5862. Tessa's remaining 24 errors were complete acoustic agreement on the same wrong transcript, involving two strings; the independently spoken repeat would have corrected all 24. Rishi example: with recording (2,4,0,3,7,3,1), source library 0 and left grouping, both hearings selected object index 2 on board 7. On board 5 they selected 3 and 1; the purchased repeat selected the correct object 3 and flipping its connector selected 2. Boards, recordings and grouping were paired; repeat choices and availability verified across initial-sound conditions. Outcomes reuse recordings and semantic libraries. The gate was engineered, reward/fees simulated and acoustic reads registered locally. Reproduction: repeat_request_audio.py VOICE 207, travel_fit.py VOICE repeat, repeat_request.py VOICE under mac.sh, then check_request.py and request_pairs.py. Per-branch raw decisions saved externally. No deployment change.

### Estimating when agreement about a spoken command is unreliable

Freeze a cross-voice, cross-form replay on the two opened repetition conditions. For each previously scored board, interpret the registered acoustic service's best candidate at every available grammatical length through the same grounded reader. Measure the excess mean acoustic cost (relative to the winning cost) of the cheapest candidate choosing another object. Fit a small decision rule on source returns: select mean or travel for immediate action, optionally ask on their action disagreement, and optionally ask when the relative gap against the mean action is at or below a threshold (-1 disabled, 0,.01,.025,.05,.10,.20,.40,.80). All eligible choices use the same repeat and .25 fee. Maximize source net, then fewer calls, smaller threshold, simpler policy. Fit on the opposite voice and the complementary half of whole operand orders (alternate in each length in fixture generation order), then aggregate held halves. No source/target sharing of whole commands within a fit; the initial acoustic templates are those of the enrolled test voice. Compare the same source selection with threshold disabled, fixed readers and the prior disagreement rule. Require positive net vs the best fixed and the same selection without gap per voice, and ≥50% recovery of Tessa's 24 consensus errors. Record whether affordable extra questions exist even with hindsight and account for false positives. Previously opened recordings; cross-fit is a diagnostic. No voice, length label, processing condition, correctness, repeat content or board seed is a policy input.

Cross-fit result per 6144: Rishi 6057 correct/26 calls/.98478 net, vs source-selected no-gap .97245 and best fixed .98177. Tessa 5788 correct/no calls/.94206, identical to source-selected no-gap and below mean-only .98486. Rishi had no wrong agreed transcripts at source, and both target Tessa folds disabled the gap. In the opened Tessa consensus subset, retrospectively asking only for <=.01 gaps would have fixed 12/24 errors in 14 calls; .05 would fix all 24 at the expense of 164 calls. The acoustic signal sometimes existed but the transfer policy missed it.

Fresh check frozen before scoring: four new operand orders at each length, lexicographically first unused permutation for each starting operand, excluding archived group/depth teaching and test orders. Cross the last two connector signs. Same graph, ears, trained readers, original paired source histories and fee. Fresh board seeds 93100–93105, initial five/ seven rates 183/273 or feature-time matched at a common geometric mean from 249 wpm, true and alternative repeats at 211. Fit once on all archived outputs of the opposite voice and compare with no-gap fit. Score the separate fixed .01 consensus extension of the original semantic gate as a post-historical hypothesis, with the identical original gate as control. Require positive net and ≥50% of consensus errors recovered on each voice where present, report false repeats and no-headroom cases. The four fresh orders per length and entire fresh board/recording cohort are excluded from policy fitting; both voices still synthetic.

Fresh orders result: learned opposite-voice gate Rishi 5794/6144 with 119 calls (.93819 net) vs best free .93278, but detected 0/260 shared errors. Tessa 6144/6144 with 50 calls (.99797) vs mean alone 6144 correct for free; no shared errors. The fixed .01 consensus extension bought 56 additional calls on Rishi and zero on Tessa, recovering no shared errors. All 260 Rishi misses would respond to a repeat; their relative gaps ranged .01479–.04303. In a post-result source comparison, applying the identical fitting procedure to the same voice's archived data selected .05 Rishi and .01 Tessa, yielding 6144/6144 correct with 751/0 calls, .96944/1 net and 260/260 Rishi shared errors corrected. Pooled data selected .01. This suggests speaker-specific calibration but the comparison was chosen after opening this split.

Confirmation: freeze all fitted policies from the original 32 archived strings (opposite-voice, same-voice, pooled, and best source fixed). Hold out the next lexicographic unused permutation per starting operand, excluding all earlier teaching and tested orders. Fresh 185/275 wpm five/seven initial, independent feature matching from 247 wpm, repeat at 203 wpm and boards 94100–94105. Evaluate same-voice calibration vs opposite-voice and strongest target fixed reader; require at least +.01 per output where shared mistakes exist and ≥50% shared-error recovery. Give full denominators even if the earlier effect disappears. No refitting on either new panel.

Independent confirmation on the second unseen-order set (32 distinct commands per voice, 6144 paired board/convention decisions each): Rishi same-voice selection reached 6059 correct after 1002 repeats, .94539 net vs opposite-voice 5760/239/.92778 and best fixed 5618/0/.91439. It corrected 291/376 (77.4%) wrong consensus decisions across five distinct commands, exceeding both prespecified recovery and net margins. The remaining 85 could also have been corrected by a repeat. Of 1002 calls, 441 corrected an otherwise wrong action and 561 left accuracy unchanged; maximum price to outperform best fixed across this mixture was 441/1002=.4401. The advantage is mixture dependent: compared to the best separate fixed reader within each condition, net differences were −.04793 fast-long and +.06112 time-matched. Tessa same-voice selection used the mean reader for all decisions, achieving 6144 correct without calls, tying best fixed; opposite-voice calibration spent 240 calls for the same accuracy. No Tessa shared errors to assess. Pooled calibration matched opposite-voice for Rishi and same-voice for Tessa. Source fitting used recorded counterfactual repeat/reader outcomes, not exclusively live chosen actions. Fees account for target repeats; source calibration acquisition and machine cost are excluded from net. Measured on two synthetic voices with repeated recordings across semantic placements. The acoustic service provided real scored candidates; the pricing and policy evaluation ran in local replay. Reproduction in external dual-role-word: gap_request.py; gap_fresh_audio.py and gap_confirm_audio.py for each voice/condition under mac.sh, travel_fit.py, corresponding request/fit scripts, audit_gap_confirm.py and confirm_breakdown.py. Verified disjoint operand orders and paired boards/receipts across initial-sound conditions. No default reader or core change.

### Learning comparative and cardinal group words from judgments

Extend prior quantifier grounding beyond selecting named quantifiers. Supply two grounded binary properties per utterance and a fixed three-word construction. For each quantifier form independently fit a small integer comparison over the four contingency cells (both, restrictor alone, predicate alone, neither). Candidate integer weights −2..2 and bias −3..3, test positive or zero, deduplicate by truth vectors on teaching menu (retain simplest), then choose the simplest consistent candidate. Teacher conventions rotate four meanings across four enrolled forms: strictly more than half, at least half, exactly one member satisfying the predicate, no such member. No distinctness requirement or supplied semantics-to-form mapping. Build a public menu with each cell 0..3 and 1..8 objects, represent each as shuffled per-object anonymous features. Two diagonal property-name combinations taught; both off-diagonal combinations withheld until testing. Buy up to 14 binary judgments per form using largest disagreement among surviving candidates, compare same-budget deterministic random lessons, whole-group counting and exact named-rule baseline. Run open continuous audio at 193 wpm teaching and 227 testing through registered scorer on Rishi and Tessa; exact tokens diagnostic. Four counterfactual conventions use identical recordings. Test restrictor sizes 4,5,8,11,16 and all possible positive counts; compare simultaneous correctness over changing spoken quantifier, changing convention, and adding unrelated predicate-positive and predicate-negative outsiders. Require ≥85% complete sets per voice and report empty groups separately (classical half-or-more is true for zero vs zero). Include a class balancing diagnostic for equality on large groups. Counts, visual property detectors, comparator grammar and teacher truth feedback are supplied. This is semantic induction within that representation.

Primary: open decoding recognized all 8 teaching and 8 held property-combination recordings per voice. Induced comparisons passed 98/98 nonempty complete sets, 6/6 exact-half sets (subset) and 2/2 empty sets per voice. Each set requires all sixteen form/convention decisions on both original and outsider-augmented scenes. Same-budget random teaching passed 36/98; whole-scene counting 0/98; named-rule baseline 98/98 with two judgments per form vs 10–13 for induction. The same semantic fixtures were used across voices. With exact hearing verified, labels from teaching belong to the observed spoken forms; no conclusion on ambiguous acoustic teaching.

Extension frozen before fitting: retain the same comparison space and acquisition budget. Switch to four meanings absent from the four named rules: strictly more than two thirds of the restricted set match, exactly two thirds match (arithmetic convention includes zero/zero), more matching objects inside than outside, and equally many matching objects inside and outside. Train on the formerly held property pairings, freeze before testing on their complement; reuse exact decoded recordings from the primary panel and use fresh teaching order/scene seeds. Test new restrictor sizes 3,6,7,10,15, outsider-positive counts 0,2,5, with and without seven further matching outsiders. Require ≥85% complete sets per voice and ≥85% joint correctness where adding outsiders actually changes the answer; compare frozen four-rule list, a refitted model forced to ignore outsiders, random teaching and exact-word diagnostic. Report original limited-menu identifiability before attributing any failure to recognition. This extension tests flexibility of the supplied comparison space; no additional acoustic claim.

Extension result: each voice passed 276/276 complete nonempty sets, 6/6 empty sets and 720/720 paired decisions on which adding outsiders changed the truth. Each form took 11–14 judgments (191 across the sixteen form/meaning fits). Matched-cost random teaching achieved 246/276 and 720/720 changes, but 0/18 complete exact-two-thirds sets; the outsider-insensitive learner scored 0/276 and the old four-rule list 18/276, neither making any correct paired outsider-sensitive change. Across both studies, the independent verifier recomputed all saved teacher answers from count algebra. Exhausting original menu-consistent candidates yielded 1–7 equivalent parameterizations per true rule; none disagreed on any of 38,416 larger count configurations (each of four cell counts 0–13). Every selected induced meaning also passed all those configurations. The 720 decisions reuse boards across form/convention forks; semantic fixtures are identical across voices. Arithmetic convention for exactly two thirds is equality of three times the numerator to twice the denominator, including zero/zero. Registered synthetic audio recognition ran locally; count induction, lesson choice and evaluation were external replay. Source and scripts under dual-role-word: quantity_audio.py, quantity_ground.py, quantity_test.py, quantity_new.py, quantity_newtest.py, verify_quantity.py with per-voice fits and judgments. No core or default package change.

### Learning scope when two spoken quantifiers share a relation

Reuse the four archived induced meanings from quantity-fit (original rotation, open hearing), each applied to a vector of Boolean values. Supply two anonymous entity sets, one observed binary relation and a fixed three-word construction with first form bound to rows and last form bound to columns. Two candidate conventions change only which quantifier takes outer scope. Teach with one spoken (strictly-most, relation, exactly-one) judgment on a 3×4 relation for which the candidates disagree. Compare withheld label, reversed valid label and a common judgment. Test all other ordered quantifier pairs, including repetitions, on 4×4 relations. Enumerate the 65536 binary graphs evaluator-side and locate conditions where two graphs with identical edge counts reverse the two scope readings in opposite directions. Preselect up to four pairs per eligible edge count and form pair (seed 101101), without using acoustic output; publish full eligibility and exclusions. Primary ≥85% complete graph × convention quartets per voice end to end and ≥20 points over swapped teaching. Open one-to-six-word hearing (single known relation form 3) at 197 wpm teaching, 229 wpm test. Record exact-word and supplied-slot diagnostics, invariant labels under row/column permutations, and common-truth controls for every ordered pair. Counts, relation matrix, lexical bindings and two possible nesting rules supplied. Identical scene sizes and edge counts preclude a size/density-only solution on quartets. These are bounded synthetic sentences; test actual dependence on graph wiring and teaching separately.

Both synthetic voices transcribed all sixteen recordings under open acoustic search; the one 3×4 distinguishing demonstration led to 280/280 complete graph × speaker quartets and 480/480 common-truth controls per voice. Shared, reversed or withheld judgment: 0/280 complete, 480/480 common. Thirteen of fifteen untrained ordered pairs admitted fully crossed equal-density examples in exhaustive 4×4 graphs. Strict-most/none and none/strict-most excluded from decisive denominator; still tested on common controls. Eligible quartet counts in lexicon order (0,1,2,7) excluding taught (0,2): 16,20,0,20,16,20,8,16,20,44,28,0,8,28,36. Independently verified every accepted label from bitwise adjacency and two successive Boolean reductions, and verified equivalent predictions after row/column permutations.

Fresh production test, before fixture generation: the sender sees a private target among three 4×4 binary relations of equal edge count and may utter one positive three-word description or defer. Sixteen acoustic candidates from the known fixed frame, enumerated generically over the two four-form slots. A listener can identify a graph iff it is the unique one satisfying the description. The sender executes its learned quantifiers and the listener's demonstrated scope to choose the first separating message in a seeded shuffled candidate order, using no graph indices or semantic labels from the evaluator. Run both matched listener conventions on the same display, recording order and target; require the sets of valid descriptions to be nonempty and disjoint (evaluator-only feasibility selection). Use first 96 qualifying seeded fixtures (102801), bounded at 50000 attempts; disclose acceptance rate. Render emitted anonymous sounds as continuous speech at 213 wpm on Rishi and Tessa; the receiver independently hears all sounds, uses its own fitted meanings and demonstrated grouping, and resolves a separately shuffled display. Compare fixed left convention sender, wrong model of listener, suppress audio after choosing and a common-description control on a separately selected 48 layouts. Include 48 isomorphic distractor trials, where no sentence can identify the target, and measure deferral. Require ≥85% both-convention success and ≥85% deferral per voice. Same vocabulary, shared relation observation and supplied truth-conditional communicative objective. Sender produces a sequence of handles through search; waveform articulation supplied by local synthesizer. This evaluates a deliberate description in a bounded grammar.

Production: retained 96 switch and 48 common-description problems from 1017 sampled triplets (14.2% retained under separate quotas, common quota reached at 713; not an unconditional prevalence estimate). In each synthetic voice the audience-aware sender changed its message in all 96 switch pairs and the receiver selected the private target in 192/192 individual branches, 96/96 complete pairs. Fixed left-convention sender 96/192 individual, 0/96 complete; wrong convention and silence each 0/192. All 48 common-description pairs succeeded, including 19 where the same message was selected. All 48 isomorphic-distractor pairs elicited deferral under both conventions. First switch fixture, private graph index 2 in sender order: (exactly-one,relation,none) for row-outer and (strict-most,relation,exactly-one) for column-outer each selected receiver index 1 after shuffling. Independent verification recomputed receiver choices from serialized graph bits and heard words. Receiver uses the other voice's separately saved semantic fits on shared training fixtures; it is enrolled in the actual speaker's acoustics. Acoustic generation/recognition is cached deterministic local synthesis and registered decoding, sender and receiver decisions are separate functions in one offline process, no live interactive trial. Referent success is conditional on the constructed finite scene menu. External scope2_audio.py, scope2_eval.py, verify_scope2.py, scope2_say.py, scope2_speak.py, verify_dialog.py and saved per-voice results. No deployment change.

### Two-way spoken clarification with privately held alternatives

Freeze a three-turn dialogue: sender sees only its private graph and chooses the first true sentence in a shared fixed utterance order; listener has a shuffled three-graph display. If two graphs remain, use truth predictions across them to choose a yes/no question. Sender hears the question and voices its answer; listener hears that answer before pointing. Use the prior learned quantifiers and two demonstrated conventions; Rishi and Tessa alternate sender/listener roles. Supply assertion/question channel and two known yes/no grounding examples per speaker under each of two swapped answer vocabularies. Fresh 221-wpm question recordings and 231-wpm replies after 189-wpm reply demonstrations. Use exhaustive graph semantics only to construct and evaluate fixtures. For each convention sample 64 fresh matched display-pairs: same initial utterance, same common possible target, different alternative graphs with disjoint sets of distinguishing questions. Cross both possible private targets per display; both actually produce the identical initial utterance. All three displayed graphs share edge count, third fails the initial assertion. Freeze seeds 104801/105801 for development/test and fixed-question selection on development only. Compare dialogue, best development-fixed question, no question, and true, withheld and inverted spoken replies with charges preserved. Include singleton and semantically indistinguishable distractor controls. Reward 1 for a correct point; charge .25 per question, and .60 on separate no-payback forks. Uniform prior on possible targets; break guess ties by display order. Require ≥85% complete four-branch success per acoustic direction/convention and ≥.15 mean net gain vs immediate guessing on cheap ambiguous trials. Report incremental economic value over the best fixed question separately. All protocols are engineered, local speech is synthetic, and utterance truth conditions come from stored scalar comparison fits.

One-question result: for each sender voice and each convention all 64/64 matched display quartets passed under both answer vocabularies (512/512 branches). Truthful/withheld/inverted reply accuracy 512/256/0 of 512 with identical fees; receiver asked different questions across every display pair. Net .75 vs .625 development-selected fixed question and .50 immediate guess. At .60 the receiver skipped all questions and scored .50. Singleton controls 128/128, no queries; indistinguishable alternatives 128/256, no queries. The supplied uniform model explicitly computes value of a split. The same semantic fixtures run with both acoustic directions. Drawing 64 test pairs took 509 and 2012 proposals in the two conventions, respectively; conditioned matched fixtures, not naturally observed question frequencies.

Two-question follow-up: four graph display, with three possible targets all independently producing identical initial assertion, plus one excluded distractor. Require all three truth signatures distinct, guaranteeing a decision tree of depth at most two with boolean questions. New held-out generation seed 106801, 64 such fixtures per convention, uniform target. Use a generic finite-horizon utility recurrence (stop and point value 1/k; a question charges the price then receives one of its predicted answers). Compare one-step and two-step policies at fees .25, .36, .45; retain actual acoustic turns and .36 withheld-second-reply branch. Predict at .36 no one-step question can repay, but two-step starts, stops after singleton reply and continues on the two-survivor reply, earning .40 per output vs 1/3 immediately. At .45 both stop. Require ≥90% all-three-correct fixture sets and ≥.05 gain at .36 per voice/convention. The reward calculation, uniform prior, depth limit and reliable-answer model are supplied rather than fitted. Check plans against an independent analytic bound and log observed branch sequences.

Two-question confirmation matched the finite-horizon bound on all truthful branches. Per acoustic direction and convention, 64/64 complete sets, 384/384 individual correct at .36 with 640 calls across both answer mappings, net .4000. One-step skipped, 128/384 and net .3333. Each three-target fork began with the same question, stopped after one particular first answer on one branch, and spent a second question on the other two branches. Suppressing just that second reply at the same fee reduced correctness to 256/384. At .25 one- vs two-step yielded .4167/.5833; at .45 both achieved .3333 and skipped. With errorless answers in this balanced fixture the two-step plan beats stopping exactly below a fee of .4 (three correct branches, five calls vs one correct unaided branch); this boundary excludes historical training/physical service costs and is not a general human conversation estimate. Local registered acoustic services plus external semantic/planning replay, with precomputed audio for a finite menu. External dialog_turn_audio.py, dialog_private.py, verify_private.py, dialog_horizon.py and verify_horizon.py in dual-role-word; independently checked ground-truth reply content, branching, expected vs realized utility and acoustic-direction replications. No deployment change.

### Learning to buy a second spoken question from experienced outcomes

Keep the same three-survivor grammar and original registered acoustic reads. Build actual question/reply transitions for fresh partner sessions. Each partner answers truthfully with probability 1 or .5 (equiprobable types); an error changes the outgoing spoken yes/no form before listener decoding. Public practice history contains eight previously verifiable yes/no answers from that partner. Use one observable feature: ≥7 of eight consistent with the known referents. No latent reliability, target identity, future outcomes or graph ID in policy. Supply the first question in seeded public order that splits remaining graphs, honest-reply filtering, and stop/ask actions; stop guesses first surviving object. Learn two-stage action values backwards from uniformly randomized stop/ask trajectories, using only chosen question costs, next public states and terminal reward. Fixed price .36; include learned one-step, history-erased learner, never ask, always continue while ambiguous, and a same-information planner estimating answer accuracy from practice. Training 98304 randomized trials on prior Rishi fixtures, with fresh independent reply and history noise; source fee/feedback cost disclosed separately. Hold out graph identities from training when generating new menus for 128 independent placements per convention; both voices reuse graphs. Test each placement in matched clean/noisy partner forks and balanced private target forks with eight noise draws; estimate paired uncertainty by resampling placements. Require ≥.015 net/output over strongest unconditional routine and learned one-step on each voice, ≥75% joint ask-clean/skip-noisy, and verify first reply changes next request. Report interference of withholding or swapping partner histories and separate comparison to planner. Fees and reliability are simulated; the question designer, representation and reliable candidate filters are supplied. Baseline controls can win.

Primary result on 256 held placements per acoustic direction: learned two-step net .35818 vs .33333 never, one-step and history-erased; always ask .09537. Paired difference vs never .02485, 95% placement bootstrap [.02236,.02716]. Opposite-history intervention .06307. Learned clean-start/noisy-stop in 5955/6144 pairs (96.9%); clean 6144/6144 correct on 10240 calls, noisy 2046/6144 on 284 calls. The same board/reply draws under both voices gave identical decisions, so they are not independent replications. Same-public-history planner using all eight counts achieved .36569; learner−planner −.00751 [−.00974,−.00544]. The learner compressed seven and eight successes into one state. Initial ask/stop learned values after high consistency .3783/.3343, truncated one-step ask .2936. Uniform exploration cost 63620 calls × .36, net 22278.8 across 98304 source trials; skipping has expected net 32768. Source deficit is larger than the observed held gains. Practice-history counts are binomial simulations of answer verification; only live question/answer forms use cached acoustic reads. No physical cost assigned to practice history.

Independent count-resolution check: fit the same chosen-action estimator on the exact observed 0..8 practice count, reproducing every original training action, cost and observed reward. No additional source feedback. Freeze new graph seeds 110003/110004 and test noise seeds 110801, matched public history, 128 placements per convention, eight repetitions and three target positions. Test partner reliabilities .50, .75, .90, .97, 1 separately; intermediate rates absent from training. Compare exact-count and compressed learner, exact-count empirical planner, skip and true-reliability planner (diagnostic upper reference under the shared filtering/action family). Refit neither policy on new outcomes. Predict exact count makes the learner stop at seven correct and start at eight, and eliminates the clean/.50 mixture deficit to the empirical planner (paired difference within ±.003). No expectation of superiority over the true-reliability reference; report unnecessary spending on unseen intermediate speakers. Practice data, reward and intervention limitations unchanged.

Exact-count fit reproduced all 161924 earlier chosen transition rows after coarsening the count. It learned to start only at eight consistent practice answers; no per-count action rule was seeded. On fresh clean/chance partners it matched the empirical planner exactly and improved net over compressed history by .00957 (paired 95% placement bootstrap [.00715,.01202]), and over never asking by .03220 [.03122,.03299]. At reliabilities .50/.75/.90/.97/1.00 full-history net per deployment .33107/.30447/.29646/.34974/.40000 vs skip .33333 throughout. Full-history total calls out of 6144 jobs per condition 33/1012/4396/8045/10240. A 90%-reliable speaker can easily pass all eight prior checks; at this price its fallible two-answer chain did not repay. The true-reliability planner skipped at .5/.75/.9 and obtained .35311/.4 at .97/1. Coarse-history net .35283 at .97, slightly better there than the more conservative exact-count learner. The fitted policy does not establish general reliability calibration or optimal communication; it is fitted in a supplied split/stop action space with error injection independent across replies. Registered speech and graph interpretation were reused; practice counts and economics simulated. External noisy_dialog.py, verify_noisy.py, noisy_count.py, verify_count.py and saved training transitions/held-out outcomes. No core or package default change.

### Grounding a spoken judgment about what the evidence permits

Teach three anonymous marker sounds on atomic link facts using true-only, false-only and mixed sets of possible observations and a binary judgment per marker/state. Fit each marker's three observed truth outputs independently (no named-modal menu). Teacher conventions rotate must, might, cannot. Then transfer to quantified claims on four-person/four-key graphs using the frozen nested quantifier meanings and learned row-outer convention. Supply the set of possible graphs, binding of atomic sound to one link, a prefix frame and a whole-proposition attachment in stage one. Teach continuous two-word marker+atomic sound at 197 wpm, evaluate four-word marker+two-quantifier clause at 233 wpm. Held clauses (0,3,2),(2,3,7),(1,3,0),(7,3,1); marker handles (4,5,6), atomic handle 3. For each clause draw 24 true/false graph pairs with equal edge count, from nontrivial cases; cross both actual completions and mask/reveal so identical accessible evidence can conceal opposite actual facts. Add same-length unanimous-true and unanimous-false alternatives. Require ≥85% complete tests across all markers, all three rotated teaching conventions, mixed and revealed evidence and unanimous controls per voice. Compare using only the first possible graph, the number of alternatives alone, rotated judgments, and exact-word diagnostic. Verify world-order invariance. Hidden actuality never supplied to prediction. This assesses transfer of a learned word over a supplied representation of possibilities, not discovering which worlds remain possible.

Stage two: atomic evidence leaves whole-proposition attachment indistinguishable from applying the modal separately to each row's property before the outer quantifier. Use both as candidate readings of the same utterance. A single compound demonstration on a separate graph pair and the first clause must distinguish them. Evaluate both conventions on the other three clauses, including only decisive pairs in the main endpoint; stratify by modal, report opportunity counts and common controls. Require ≥85% both-convention accuracy and ≥20-point gain over a common-target demonstration. Independent fixture seeds 112901, 113901; synthetic voices and generator are shared across conventions. Report acoustic and semantic results separately.

Primary result: Tessa 96/96 complete sets and 864/864 decisive both-attachment judgments after the compound example (three rotated vocabularies on 288 placements), vs 0 for shared/reversed compound lesson. Rishi 0/96 and 384/864; all twelve compounds heard exactly, but atomic lesson (5,3) was decoded as (4,5,3), leaving one marker entirely ungrounded. Known-slot or exact tokens recovered 96/96 and exact tokens 864/864. First-world-only and rotated whole-marker judgments 0/96 complete; world-count-only cannot pass any set because two equally sized unanimous observations can require opposite answers. All nine held clause × marker-meaning strata had decisive pairs. The atomic support code encodes presence of true and false completions; learning maps those three support states to judgments.

Follow-up fixed before new audio: ask for one repetition whenever the heard sequence fails the supplied atomic or compound frame. Use repeat at 209 wpm on original recordings. Then evaluate entirely fresh recordings of the same strings with first atomic/compound rates 211/249 and a 217-wpm repeat. Compare open without repeat, validity-triggered repeat, always repeat and an evaluator length diagnostic. No ground-truth identities to trigger repeat, no search for a favorable synthesis speed; report remaining valid-but-wrong sequences and counts of repeat requests per distinct presented recording. Keep semantic fits, lessons, controls and paired board metrics unchanged. The tests use the same syntactic frames previously supplied to semantic interpretation and repeat charges are counts rather than a net return.

Rishi validity-gated repeat on the original panel bought one of fifteen recordings and still misheard (5,3); fresh panel bought two, repaired one compound but not the atomic phrase. Both whole-set scores remained 0/96 and attachment 384/864. Always repeat also failed, while oracle word count with no slot restrictions gave 15/15 exact and 96/96, 864/864 on both panels. Tessa requested no repeats and remained perfect.

Alternative lesson fixed before fitting: when a compound marker handle is heard but has no lexical judgments from the atomic lessons, ask a teacher for three judgments using that sound in a new carrier (marker,0,3,7) at 201 wpm. Use all-empty-link and all-present-link graphs in true-only, false-only and mixed support states. All rows have the same predicate within either graph, so both attachment sites give the same answer for this strict-majority outer quantifier under any of the three teacher markers. Decode the carrier by open hearing; infer the support code from the archived quantifier parameters applied to its decoded inner clause. Keep all accepted atomic associations. Compare missing labels vs true and other teacher-rotation labels, report extra queries and acoustic exactness. Evaluate on the fresh 211/249 panel with the already tested syntax-triggered 217-wpm repeat in every arm. The alternate carrier does not occur on any test. This is an engineered lesson format and a test of learning from a different use of the same sound; compare with the known-length baseline from above.

Alternative carrier recovery: all three new carrier recordings decoded exactly per voice. Rishi requested three judgments for the single missing marker in each rotated installation. With the same fresh acoustic input and graph fixtures, complete certainty/possibility sets rose from 0 to 96/96, and both-attachment success from 384 to 864/864 decisive pairs. Alternative valid teacher rotation for just the extra labels gave 0/96 and 410/864, respectively. Every previously accepted other-marker decision stayed identical. Tessa requested no further judgments and retained 96/96 and 864/864 across interventions. The extra lesson uses three truth labels with a single new recording, not three new speakers or an improvement in boundary detection. Enumeration of possible graphs, prefix grammar, Boolean support representation and two attachment sites supplied. External possible_audio.py, possible.py, verify_possible.py, possible_repeat_audio.py, possible_length.py, possible_views.py, possible_follow.py, possible_carrier_audio.py, possible_recovery.py and saved raw fits/decisions in dual-role-word. ### Inferring a speaker's possible graphs from observation histories

Keep the recovered modal lexicons, quantified truth functions and whole-proposition attachment frozen. Remove the supplied list of possible worlds at test. Supply a binary 4×4 relation universe and the nine relative cell features of a wrapped 3×3 observation window. A sensor reports an integer. Fit an affine readout by least squares from 48 publicly verified local observations on random 3×3 training graphs (seed 114901). Two installations share all training windows, but the readout adds the central bit and its right or lower neighbour; no candidate offset pair or exact coefficients supplied to the fit. Validate on unseen 4×4 locations and enumerate compatible graphs using the learned readings only. Test identical public histories of 4–12 distinct probe locations and readings under the two fitted sensor installations, selecting valid histories in which both have possible graphs and the clause's truth support differs. Equal sampling of the four previously spoken clauses, 64 matched histories per clause, budget 24000 proposals, seed 115901. No actual graph/labels in listener path. Require ≥85% complete sets across both sensor installations, all three modal sounds and all three marker rotations on each voice. Report opportunities and failures, cardinalities and whether true-only, false-only or mixed. Compare erased calibration (union over both still possible readouts), wrong calibration, and last observation only. Independent verifier checks full membership against bitwise physical sensor constraints. This tests inference from supplied local geometry and an explicit finite universe, not learning a belief representation from pixels.

From 4428 candidate draws, 64 held matched histories per clause, 256 total. Both voices completed 256/256 with fitted readouts, 0/256 with union over sensors or swapped calibration, 10/256 with last reading only. Of 512 histories across both installations the compatible-graph counts ranged 1–4096. Support contrasts row vs column: false-only/mixed 188, mixed/false-only 40, true-only/mixed 21, mixed/true-only 4, true-only/false-only 2, reverse 1. Explicitly verified learned constraints against independent bit-index calculations at every location for all 65536 graphs and both sensors. Basis and wrap geometry supplied, learned weights frozen.

Prospective active observation: two different familiar spoken quantified claims on the same partially observed graph, same installed observation function, observations and legal locations. Task reward 1 for a correct definite report, 0 for an honest unresolved report, −1 for a wrong definite report; cost .25 to take one reading. Supply equal prior mass per compatible graph and a one-step planner. Score a probe by the expected fraction of possible outcomes (weighted by world count) after which the clause is settled. Use recovered modal words for must when all completions satisfy the claim, cannot when none do, and might for mixed (a supplied reporting convention). Compare maximum observation-outcome entropy with a stop option when its selected probe cannot repay, a uniformly sampled unobserved probe and stopping. Generate fresh histories with 4–10 observed sites (seed 116901) and a uniform physical graph; evaluator selects 48 paired cases per sensor with both claims mixed and different, disjoint sets of strictly profitable best locations, and 32 mixed cases per sensor where no probe repays. Cap at 20000 proposals per sensor. Keep every possible actual graph represented using outcome multiplicities. Require ≥85% both-target optimal choices, ≥.08 net gain over stopping and ≥.02 over cost-aware entropy per sensor on paired cases; report no-payback separately. Reconstruct full emit/hear/report using archived 4-word open readings after validated repetition, and preserve honest final classification if a paid observation is withheld. This tests an engineered value-of-information calculation operating on learned semantics and dynamics, not learned inquiry optimization.

Active probe result: 48/48 paired target switches per sensor/voice; both selected locations independently verified optimal against all unobserved sites using explicit compatible IDs, physical sensor arithmetic and separately evaluated clause truth. Mean net/output on row-sensor matched cases .54688 vs .19792 entropy, −.01165 random and 0 stop; column .35417 vs .10156, .02999 and 0. Withhold the observed reading after purchase: unresolved report throughout, −.25 net. All branch-weighted spoken statuses were correct across both voices. Both value-based and cost-aware entropy policies skipped all 32 no-payback cases per sensor (these are clause/history cases and need not have distinct histories). Reached both quotas after 171/222 proposed histories. All switch endpoints are conditional on having different profitable optimal probes; these numbers do not estimate deployment frequency. Under this reward, a definite truthful answer is preferred to the permitted cautious report. Local registered acoustic decoding reused, inference/observation model and simulated fees evaluated externally. Scripts possible_sensor.py, verify_sensor.py, freeze_modal.py, modal_probe.py and verify_modal_probe.py and per-voice raw branch predictions saved in dual-role-word. No core or deployment change.

### Learning what a speaker implies by choosing a truthful modal word

Two speaker habits share the grounded modal lexicon: both say cannot for false-only support and might for mixed support. For true-only support one uses must while the other uses might. Supply a deterministic stable choice per support state; initialize allowed outputs from the grounded lexical truth tables, then retain the heard choice on a verified calibration situation. Use previously recovered acoustic readings and learned sensor/quantifier fits. Calibration reports concern only clause 0; compare a separating fully known positive graph, a redundant mixed case, and reversing only the informative calibration report. On each deployment six distinct equiprobable graphs share edge count. Observer makes one private sensor reading at a public location; listener sees only the speaker's spoken report. Invert that choice to infer which graphs remain possible. Test same heard might on the two habits; predict a *different* second clause about the actual graph. Freeze 48 matched cases per installed sensor, seed 117901+s, max 100000 proposals; initial clause drawn from remaining three, second different. Require both reports attainable and exact conditional probabilities strictly on opposite sides of .5. Score joint MAP decisions and Brier loss, weighting physical graphs by their actual conditional frequency. Controls: literal interpretation with uniform surviving graphs, erased preference, wrong calibration, and a same-information deterministic enumerator. Include unconditioned random deployments, 500 fixtures per sensor, without selecting for a change; score every emitted report under each habit. Require ≥85% joint switch and ≥.02 Brier improvement vs literal on matched cases per voice; report population control separately. Literal semantics, finite common prior, knowledge of the observer's access and production stability are supplied. No listener access to actual graph or private receipt. Multiple worlds and habits reuse the same sound file.

First deterministic-habit study: both voices made the correct majority prediction in both matched speaker forks on 48/48 per sensor; literal, redundant-history and swapped-history controls 0/48. Exact conditional Brier (lower better) attentive vs literal: .22463 vs .27477 row, .22389 vs .27569 column. The independent bitwise enumerator exactly matched each learned posterior. The switch fixtures were rare (48 in 29225 and 36043 proposals); on 500 unselected six-graph displays the Brier gains were only .00014/.00011. No general economic advantage claimed.

Probabilistic extension: when certain a clause is true, speaker voices might with probability theta=.2 or .8; otherwise previous mixed/false-only production stays deterministic. Use 0,1,4,16,64 verified certain-positive spoken calibration judgments per partner; learn a rate with a uniform Beta(1,1) prior. Fit only to actual correctly heard must/might reports. Compare literal support, equal-weight fixed theta=.5, a deterministic first-example commitment, and an oracle knowing theta. Twenty-four independent nested practice histories per habit/voice, common uniforms for matched speakers, seed 119201. Test only clauses absent from calibration, on fresh 6-graph displays and partner-access readings using seeds 118901+s. Reserve 64 cases per sensor where both target theta values give opposite strict-majority decisions after identical might, cap 150000 proposals; separately report 500 unfiltered displays with all emissions weighted at their actual probability. Freeze target: 16-example posterior must recover at least half the Brier headroom over fixed .5 on both matched panels, and improve over one-example commitment per sensor. Cover zero-headroom displays, report percent paired correct peaks without removing near-ties. Supply stationary conditional frequency model and uniform prior; no inference-time theta, private graph or private reading. This isolates the benefit of estimating occasional hedging, not acquiring the concept of honesty.

Probabilistic confirmation: 64 switch cases per sensor reached after 42176/47886 draws. Mean over 24 practice histories and both true habits: after 16 verified examples posterior Brier .242128/.242020 versus equal-weight .249228/.249196, first-example commitment .260501/.260635 and true-rate oracle .241036/.240914. This recovered 86.7%/86.6% of available improvement on matched cases. Both matched decisions correct on 85.35%/86.91% of history×case pairs at 16, 98.24%/98.89% at 64. On the 500 unfiltered displays per sensor at 16 the absolute reduction vs fixed .5 was only .000034/.000082. Hence clear cue-sensitive comprehension conditional on useful cases, little average need on the random workload. Concrete deterministic example: identical observed might for clause (2,3,7), same six possibilities and same secondary clause (0,3,2), posterior 2/3 under strongest-report habit vs 2/5 under cautious habit. The same utterance is literally true under both. Acoustic directions shared fixtures and produced identical outcomes. The stationary production probabilities, sensor access, probability update and prior over candidate diagrams are supplied; no spontaneous development of conversational norms claimed. Scripts report_choice.py, verify_report_choice.py, hedge_rate.py and verify_hedge.py (with per-voice raw results) in external dual-role-word. No deployment change.

### Discovering which public context changes a speaker's modal choice

Keep prior grounded modal words, learned sensor and quantified clause semantics. When known certain, speaker hedges with probability .2 or .8 according to one of two visible anonymous binary cues (recipient identity or a badge); cross the governing cue and its polarity. Constant-rate .5 control. Both features equally frequent; in initial practice they coincide, in later practice vary independently. Give listener a common candidate library (constant, either single partition, XOR), equal prior over partitions and independent uniform Beta priors per bin. Learn posterior mixture from public cue values and heard must/might on verified certain-positive practice reports; governing cue, polarity and actual rates withheld. Compare pooled, always-recipient, always-badge and oracle. Frozen 64 histories per world, 32 balanced diagonal training examples then 0,4,16,64 balanced crossed (off-diagonal) examples, seed 120701. For matched opposite governing cue worlds share initial diagonal practice exactly. Probe on the two off-diagonal public contexts with the same speaker, report audio, graph menu and target question; target clauses absent from practice. Use fresh matched sensor fixtures (121901+s), previous compact posterior/Brier tests, 64 switch cases/sensor plus 500 unfiltered fixtures; same 150000-draw cap. Require identical pre-cross predictions across hidden worlds, ≥75% joint off-diagonal decisions and ≥50% recovery of Brier headroom over pooled after 64 crossing judgments per sensor; measure earlier checkpoints, stationary control, wrong-cue and empty-headroom cases separately. Cue features and the partition family supplied; conditional reporting probabilities and context selection inferred. Results concern synthetic classroom conventions.

Matched sensor panels contained 64 cases each after 34647/48779 proposals. Before crossings the two hidden-cue histories and mixture predictions were identical, 0% joint off-diagonal decisions. Joint results at 4/16/64 crossing judgments: 74.06/93.32/98.60% row, 73.07/92.55/98.41% column. At 64 the mixture recovered 95.5%/95.4% of Brier headroom over pooling. Stationary .5 arm: small Brier penalties vs pooling (.000126/.000130 on selected cases at 64). Unfiltered context-dependent gains .000048/.000008 vs pooling at 64; no broad utility claim. The fixed always-recipient and always-badge models improved with off-diagonal training but had greater loss than the mixture.

Compositional context confirmation: three anonymous binary public cues, two withheld settings (001 and 110). Learn partitions by enumerating Boolean functions over subsets of zero, one or two features and deduplicating complementary truth vectors; equal prior per resulting partition and Beta(1,1) outcome model per bin. Supply no governing cue pair or polarity. The speaker tends to hedge on the conjunction of the first two canonical bits both 1 (or both 0 in a matched world), with .8 in the exceptional bin and .2 elsewhere. Relabel and permute features for each practice history; hold both target configurations outside calibration. Both rates and partition must be estimated. Compare earlier single-cue-plus-XOR library generalized to three inputs, fully independent Beta rate per context, and same-information two-feature oracle given only the partition. Uniform prior over true habits is not passed to policies. Freeze 48 histories, 4/16/64 examples per observed context, seed 122701; score previously reserved sensor fixtures separately as an opened acoustic/semantic benchmark, using no downstream labels to fit. Require ≥85% both-held-context correct decisions and at least half of available Brier improvement over independent unseen-context lookup after 64 per context on both sensors. Before scoring, audit identifiability with exact six-context population rates within the two-feature family. Add an observationally identical three-feature exception changing only the two withheld rates; report failure before a new target observation, without implying that transfer can infer a hidden exception.

Compositional result on the opened 64 switch fixtures per sensor: after 4/16/64 judgments in each of six teaching contexts, joint correctness on both unheard contexts was 45.9/94.1/100% for the row sensor and 46.5/94.7/100% for the column sensor. At 64 this is 6144/6144 paired decisions per sensor (48 practice histories × 2 habits × 64 shared fixtures). Relative to assigning .5 to the unknown reporting rates, the two-feature learner recovered 98.7% of available Brier improvement for each sensor. The narrower single-feature/XOR library's Brier scores were .24945/.24935 versus .24126/.24090 for the expanded library. On 500 unfiltered displays per sensor the absolute Brier gains over .5 were only .000048/.000010. Both synthetic voice replays used the same semantic draws and agreed exactly. On the exception forks the calibration observations and listener predictions stayed identical while the two true held-context rates exchanged places. At 64 neither sensor produced a single jointly correct pair; a neutral .5 guess had better Brier loss than the confident transfer. This identifies dependence on the supplied two-feature model family. Scripts context_hedge.py, verify_context.py, hedge_composition.py and verify_composition.py and per-voice results in the external dual-role-word archive. No deployed model change.

### Pricing one spoken example before transferring a reporting habit

Use the opened 64 switch and 500 unfiltered six-graph displays per sensor. Before seeing a report, offer a verified certain-positive calibration report from the new context, at fee .02, then one or 32 independently drawn jobs with the same public display and clauses. Supply balanced regular/exception prior, where exception exchanges the two held-context rates. Infer the two rates from six previously seen contexts using the frozen composition learner (64 observations/cell, 48 histories, two polarities). Marginalize over every future message including must and cannot, every graph, both speaker regimes and both actual calibration receipts. Each scored probability costs squared prediction error. Freeze one-step expected loss reduction and compare buy-always, skip, unused paid receipt, inverted receipt and an oracle with true rates but no advance regime knowledge. Require positive net gain over both fixed routines on an equal mix of horizons for each sensor on switch displays; report unfiltered and each horizon separately. This is a public-information planner with supplied exception model and recurrence, testing economic feasibility before fitting an inquiry policy.

Both voices produced identical results on shared semantic fixtures. Mean cumulative gain over skipping at horizon 1 was zero: no purchases in either cohort. At horizon 32 on 64 switch displays per sensor (96 fitted practice histories each), purchase rates were 99.12%/99.41%, with net gains .04083/.04325 per deployment. Always buying at 32 slightly exceeded the fitted planner (.04099/.04337) but lost .01809/.01802 on single jobs. Equal mixture over horizons: selective advantage over skip .02041/.02163 and over always buying .00895/.00896. Reversing the paid utterance's interpretation at the same price gave −.20438/−.21199 at horizon 32; suppressing it gave −.01982/−.01988. With rates known and regime hidden the planner could gain .04203/.04444 at horizon 32. Across 500 unfiltered displays per sensor, long-horizon purchase rates were only .773%/.213%, and net gains .000137/.000004. These are exact branch-weighted risks using archived acoustic reads, with repeated work and a balanced exception prior supplied and no new live teacher calls. Independent recomputation of oracle benefit by summing the physical speaker regimes matched the planning calculation to 1e−12 per display. Costs are simulated Brier units, excluding historical learning and local compute. External price_modal.py and price-modal-{rishi,tessa}.json. This establishes economic headroom conditional on the workload and model assumptions; the choice rule was supplied.

Random exploration bought 4077/8192 examples for 81.54 units; realized cumulative gain after fees relative to scoring the saved pre-example reports on the same jobs was −9.193. The independently refitted pricing coefficient was .185137. Mean held net improvement over skipping across the balanced benchmark: learned .01044/.01090, long-horizon-only .00553/.00596, always ask −.00399/−.00354, public fitted-rate planner .01042/.01089. Paired stratified display-bootstrap 95% intervals vs long-horizon-only [.00486,.00496]/[.00490,.00497]. Both prespecified comparisons passed. Near equality to the planner is on shared fitted-rate assumptions with finite histories; its fitted rates are imperfect. At 32 jobs the learner bought on 99.38%/99.63% of switch displays and .454%/.452% of unfiltered displays averaged over practice fits. No short deployments bought. Reversing the meaning of a paid utterance gave −.05195/−.05337 overall. The unfiltered-only mean gain over skipping (balanced horizons) was .000025/.000050. Same fitted speaker histories, synthetic grammar and speaker dynamics across source and target; separate generated graph displays, with the switch stratum intentionally enriched. Intervals describe resampling displays within strata conditional on this one fitted policy and reused histories, not independent speakers or training runs. No prospective whole-study break-even measured. Acoustic reads reused; action and outcome learning in external replay. Scripts learn_modal_price.py and check_learn_modal.py, chosen-action summaries and per-display target measurements under dual-role-word. Syntax-assisted reanalysis changed exactly three Rishi readings and zero Tessa readings. Both then heard 14/14 strings exactly. Each achieved 96/96 complete mixed sets after the separating judgment, 0/96 after a common or absent judgment (384/768 individual correct), and 0/768 individual after the opposite judgment; all 192 unanimous singleton sets survived every receipt intervention. Verified per-scene counts and equal edge counts for each graph pair in both saved replays. Only 12 distinct held utterances per voice, sharing synthetic forms and deterministic semantics. One reader learned four states from 40 sequences (120 binary judgments), the other six states from 58 (174 judgments); longest teaching prefix had four operators. Known length and anonymous slot alphabets gave 24/24 exact and 12/12 complete pairs on both voices. There are twelve selected reversal pairs and three distinct held base clauses. The variable-length filter did not recover a single additional complete reversal pair even with three available. ### When a second recording changes a modal judgment

Freeze the learned scope machines, acoustic weights and inferred frame. Equal weight per utterance, convention and support condition (singleton false, singleton true, mixed); 144 scored decisions per voice. All recordings rendered offline for controlled evaluation, and only requested repeat enters the selective policy. No learned repeat selector claimed.

Rishi 130/144 correct with either free reader, 133/144 after 26 meaning-triggered calls; recovered 3 of 14 errors, lost zero, missing the 50% criterion. Transcript-triggered calls gave 134/144 after 54 calls, unconditional fusion 134/144 after 144. Net/output at .05 was .91458 selective, .91181 transcript, .88056 unconditional, .90278 free. Selective break-even vs either free reader is 3/26=.11538 per call on this mixture. Pair-cluster bootstrap interval for selective minus base [−.0080,.0340], 12 reversal pairs; benefit uncertain. Withhold or swap the paid repeat: 130/144 and .89375 with unchanged charges. All three recovered judgments were on mixed evidence. Always-repeat fusion could fix only four of the fourteen baseline errors. The ten errors that survived fusion came from two nine-word recordings: their correct transcripts ranked second and third, below four- and six-word hearings respectively. Tessa had 144/144 before and after, zero meaning-triggered calls (18 transcript-triggered); no headroom for a positive gain. Across first recordings, exact transcripts base/travel/fusion were 18/20/22 of 24 Rishi, 24/21/24 Tessa. Independent support-table check agreed on all cases where the alternative readings kept the true clause (138 and 144 branches). Recordings are synthetic and shared across scene/convention forks. ### Counting recurrent sounds by their internal acoustic trajectory

Fit separate one-state and three-successive-state models per enrolled sound from isolated forms and the same 56 short labeled connected recordings used for acoustic calibration. Initialize ordered centroids from equal thirds of the isolated sound, align through the supplied training transcript with stay/advance transitions, then re-estimate centroids and geometric durations for six rounds. Global feature standardization, unit emission variance, no target labels, true count or rate used in ranking. Rerank the frozen candidates on the opened 24-utterance repeat panel from the first hearing alone. Require recovery of ≥7 of Rishi's 14 judgment errors with ≤1 formerly correct judgment lost per voice. Separately synthesize each of three operator forms repeated 1–4 times within two contrasting fixed carriers at 187 and 241 wpm. Provide the four possible transcripts in this count-isolation diagnostic; require ≥80% exact count and ≥10 percentage point advantage vs one-state on both voices, reporting complete count quartets. Distinguish the constrained count diagnostic from open candidate reranking. Preregister before fitting.

Primary count-isolation result: three ordered states 42/48 correct and 10/12 complete quartets on each voice, one state 12/48 and zero quartets each. To distinguish acoustic trajectory from utterance duration, freeze fitted parameters and independently resample all four frame sequences within each carrier/form/rate quartet to their common rounded mean frame count. Re-evaluate both models and add a within-recording temporal shuffle with the same length and frame multiset, seed 130801. These are feature-level interventions, with a common duration set evaluator-side, not additional natural recordings. Predict ≥75% correct with ordered equal-length inputs and ≥15 points over permuted inputs for the three-state reader on each voice.

Open reranking from first audio alone on the previous repeat placements: Rishi base/one-state/three-state exact 18/0/10 of 24 and meaning correct 130/101/108 of 144. The three-state reader recovered ten base errors but lost 32 correct judgments, failing retention. Tessa exact 24/11/20 with all 144 judgments correct in every arm. Constrained count by sound (forms 1,4,5): Rishi 16/10/16 of 16; Tessa 13/16/13. Equalizing frame count within quartets still yielded 42/48 each voice. Permuting those same frames dropped three-state accuracy to 14/48 each, versus 12/48 one-state throughout. The per-voice totals coincide despite different per-form errors. Independently recomputed 48 saved trajectory costs per voice with a backward recursion. These are synthetic continuous recordings; the constrained quartet provides alternative full strings and changes only multiplicity. Equality of total duration controls one shortcut; the scramble also disrupts within-word spectral transitions. Training uses known form identities and transcripts, registered acoustic features, external Viterbi fitting and scoring. No deployed change. ### Selective acoustic repair of a single repeated run

Freeze the three-state acoustic model and original reader. For a base reading, admit only its existing acoustic candidates whose run-length encodings have identical ordered symbols and differ in at most one count. Compare this restricted reranker with the unrestricted three-state reader and the unchanged base. Test a separate proposal intervention: vary each run of the base reading to any positive count, keeping length 3–10 and the inferred frame legal, and rank all such one-run edits plus the base. Do not pass the intended scaffold, run location or true length. Compare the single-state model over the same expanded set. First audit the opened 24-utterance panel, then test two new carriers per repeated operator, counts 1–4 and rates 193/235, 48 recordings per voice. Score exact hearing and downstream judgments from frozen readers. Primary criterion on new audio: recover ≥50% of base transcription errors with ≤1 loss per voice for expanded three-state, report no headroom explicitly. Keep proposal coverage, conditional ranking and semantic consequences distinct.

Fresh result: base/restricted/expanded exact 42/45/45 of 48 Rishi and 48/48/31 Tessa. Expanding the menu thus failed the Tessa retention criterion by 17 losses. Restricting to existing candidates fixed 3/6 Rishi errors and lost none; Tessa had no initial errors or losses. For the 44 recordings per voice with strings absent from the earlier constrained count diagnostic, restricted exact 38→41 and 44→44; two of 24 strings overlapped that diagnostic under different rates. To test retention beyond count-focused carriers, replay the frozen restricted and expanded arms on all 82 earlier acoustic-travel recordings per voice with identical archived candidate lists; report each cohort, correct and spoiled transcripts, and scene judgments. These are opened regression guards, not fresh confirmation. No tuning.

Fresh semantic totals (288 decisions per voice): base/restricted/expanded 270/280/274 Rishi and 288/288/195 Tessa. All three eligible Rishi transcription errors were repaired; three other errors required more than changing one run of the base. Opened repeat panel restricted 18→19/24 exact and 130→135/144 meaning Rishi, 24→24 and 144→144 Tessa. On the other 82 guards restricted Rishi exact 68→70 and no correct transcript spoiled (19→20, 31→31, 18→19), Tessa 82→82. Nevertheless Rishi guard meaning totals were 141→141, 198→198, 134→129; in a single command an already incorrect four-word hearing changed to an incorrect five-word hearing, changing 5/6 accidentally correct judgments to 0/6. The correct eight-word candidate existed outside the single-run neighborhood. This rules out general semantic non-interference even on panels with zero loss of exact transcripts. Exhaustive candidate reranking used external dynamic programming on registered acoustic features. Independently checked edit membership by deletion, original candidate coverage and base alignment costs. ### Disambiguating a heard instruction with one speaker judgment

Freeze the semantic machines and variable-length candidate generator. Estimate reliability by acoustic rank with unit pseudocounts from the 58 old+variable public labeled recordings per voice. First synthesis 181/251 wpm. Select the scene whose yes/no partition of plausible hearings best reduces expected future decision errors over the preview. Price .15, nominal 1 or 8 future uses. The actual 128 test scenes per placement are hidden from question selection. Compare skip, base hearing, always choosing the best scene, spending-matched uniform random question (average over menu), suppression and inversion. Use archived acoustically decoded yes/no forms. Score net gain in expected correct outputs after the fee and paid-receipt effect; preregister +.02 net per eight-job deployment over skip and random and identify headroom if either voice fails. The teacher judges the entire intended utterance in the displayed scene; no token, boundary or transcript returned. Planner and finite scene generator supplied.

Fresh results (32 recordings × two conventions, 128 independent future-scene draws per placement, shared synthetic scene draws across voices). For Rishi at eight uses, selective 7.7682 net vs skip 7.3926, matched random 7.7524, always-best 7.7611; 61/64 purchases, five improved gross accuracy, none reduced it. Inverting the paid yes/no fell to .7916, suppressing it to 7.2496. Paired 95% prefix-pair bootstrap intervals: vs skip +.3756 [−.1406,1.0916], vs random +.0158 [−.0248,.0691]; the .02 targeted-choice margin failed. Only six placements had variation across the twelve possible lessons' actual accuracies; same-spend hindsight advantage over random was .1047. Nine placements had any lesson worth its cost; omniscient buy-or-skip gain upper bound .5863 over skip. At one use, 11/64 buys and net .9452 vs .9241 skip; random tied. Tessa's skip was perfect; no lesson could pay at either horizon. At eight uses the planner bought 46/64, net 7.8922 vs 8 skip and identical random. At one use it skipped throughout. Tessa's 58 source top ranks were all correct, yet Laplace smoothing puts 7/66 prior mass on lower ranks in an eight-candidate hearing; other ranks do predict different truths. Both voices had the true hearing in all 32 lists. Every decision charge and random-menu expectation was checked; an independent error enumeration verified the expected-gain planner on 50 synthetic hypothesis tables. Results demonstrate potential for meaning clarification on a fallible reader, without reliable superiority of this scene-selection or stopping policy. Registered local hearing for new commands; archived decoded binary reply forms, external semantic replay with supplied question interface and transparent truth teacher. No runtime deployment changes. Use exactly the 58 old/variable labeled recordings per voice; no target lessons for fitting. At H=1 Rishi old/hier/gap net .89944/.87305/.90181 on 14/0/12 calls; Tessa no purchases and net 1 throughout. Thus the prespecified net point margin cleared but the calibration prediction failed. A post-primary crossed replay of gate/interpretation gave 7.84551 gap gate with hierarchical interpretation and 7.81523 hierarchical gate with gap interpretation. Tessa Brier old/hier/gap .01125/.000072/.000036 on a panel with zero errors. Thresholds, fee and checks conditional on two synthetic voices; no general trust or economic training break-even claim.
